Hi. Um… I think what finally made me make the appointment is that I noticed I went from barely being able to just settle into my new apartment—like, boxes still around, basic things not really organized—to suddenly saying yes to everything.

Not just update it a little—like redesign the whole thing, rethink my case studies, fix every project, make it all perfect. It felt like I had to fix my entire life in one sprint, if that makes sense.

At first it felt productive, like maybe I was finally getting on top of things, but then I kind of stepped back and thought, okay, this is… a lot. I hadn’t even really unpacked properly, and suddenly I was stacking all these goals and commitments on myself like they were urgent. That’s what brought me in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. That was actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, something is off, because normally if I only sleep a few hours, I’m useless the next day. But during that stretch, there were several nights where I slept maybe three or four hours—like I’d go to bed really late because I was working on something or thinking through what I wanted to do next—and then I’d wake up really early and feel like, “Great, I’m up, I should use this time.”

It didn’t feel like being tired and pushing through it. It felt more like I genuinely believed I was fine, like I didn’t need more sleep and it would be wasteful not to get ahead while I had the energy. Looking back, that’s not really normal for me.

flight for a trip I couldn't really afford, and only later did I realize how badly it hit my savings.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I missed a design ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think so. Not cheerful in a normal “I’m having a good week” way, exactly. Earlier this month there were moments where I felt almost electric, like everything was suddenly possible and I was just… bright, or switched on. I remember feeling really upbeat, like I could handle anything and people should just get on board because I had momentum.

But it wasn’t consistently pleasant, which is part of what confused me. I’d be in that really energized, optimistic state, and then if something tiny slowed me down, I’d get sharply irritated. Like I snapped at a contractor over a delay that, looking back, was not that dramatic. And I snapped at my sister too—over something small, just not responding fast enough or not understanding what I was trying to do—and in the moment it felt like they were personally blocking me, like they were obstacles in my way.

So… yes, there was this unusually upbeat feeling, but it also tipped really quickly into irritability. It felt excessive. Not like my usual mood.

It felt more like I genuinely believed I was fine, like I didn’t need more sleep and it would be wasteful not to get ahead while I had the energy. Looking back, that’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t remember people saying, like, “You’re talking too much” or anything that obvious. I may have been more… animated? Like when I was talking about the portfolio redesign or client stuff, I could go on for a while because I had all these connected ideas in my head and I wanted to explain the whole chain of why each thing mattered.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as talking more than usual, or if I was just excited and kind of over-explaining. I do that sometimes anyway, especially with design work, because I’ll start with one thought and then realize there are five related pieces of context. So… maybe a little? But I can’t say for sure that I was noticeably more talkative in a way other people pointed out.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or almost excessively cheerful?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not cheerful in a normal “I’m having a good week” way, exactly. Earlier this month there were moments where I felt almost electric, like everything was suddenly possible and I was just… bright, or switched on. I remember feeling really upbeat, like I could handle anything and people should just get on board because I had momentum.\n\nBut it wasn’t consistently pleasant, which is part of what confused me. I’d be in that really energized, optimistic state, and then if something tiny slowed me down, I’d get sharply irritated. Like I snapped at a contractor over a delay that, looking back, was not that dramatic. And I snapped at my sister too—over something small, just not responding fast enough or not understanding what I was trying to do—and in the moment it felt like they were personally blocking me, like they were obstacles in my way.\n\nSo… yes, there was this unusually upbeat feeling, but it also tipped really quickly into irritability. It felt excessive. Not like my usual mood.\n\n[Last Question]\nAbout how many days in a row did that unusually energized, upbeat-or-irritable period last?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

It’s hard to pin it down to an exact number of days, because it didn’t feel like one clean block with a clear start and stop. But the whole intense up-and-down pattern has basically been going on since I moved into the apartment, so… about a month.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it has. I’m kind of embarrassed to say that, because part of me wants to say I’ve been “handling it,” but that’s not really true.

Work definitely took a hit. I missed a design review, which is not like me. I mean, I’m usually the person who’s overly aware of calendars and deadlines, and this time I just… I don’t know, I was scattered enough that I didn’t show up the way I was supposed to. And it wasn’t some tiny optional meeting either—it mattered. Afterward I felt awful, like I’d made myself look unreliable or unprofessional.

And with relationships, yes. I’ve been avoiding friends and family more than usual. Not because I don’t care about them, but because I feel embarrassed. I feel like I’ve been so all over the place lately—hard to pin down, intense, unreliable, kind of scattered—and I don’t want to have to explain it or see their reaction. So I’ve let messages sit, I’ve put off calls, and then the longer I avoid people, the more ashamed I feel.

So, yes. I wouldn’t say my life completely fell apart, but it’s caused real problems.

Um… I think what finally made me make the appointment is that I noticed I went from barely being able to just settle into my new apartment—like, boxes still around, basic things not really organized—to suddenly saying yes to everything.\n\nI’m a web and interface designer, and it was like out of nowhere I was agreeing to mentoring sessions, taking on extra client prototype work, and then deciding I needed to completely redo my entire portfolio at the same time. Not just update it a little—like redesign the whole thing, rethink my case studies, fix every project, make it all perfect. It felt like I had to fix my entire life in one sprint, if that makes sense.\n\nAt first it felt productive, like maybe I was finally getting on top of things, but then I kind of stepped back and thought, okay, this is… a lot. I hadn’t even really unpacked properly, and suddenly I was stacking all these goals and commitments on myself like they were urgent. That’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: During this period, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, something is off, because normally if I only sleep a few hours, I’m useless the next day. But during that stretch, there were several nights where I slept maybe three or four hours—like I’d go to bed really late because I was working on something or thinking through what I wanted to do next—and then I’d wake up really early and feel like, “Great, I’m up, I should use this time.”\n\nIt didn’t feel like being tired and pushing through it. It felt more like I genuinely believed I was fine, like I didn’t need more sleep and it would be wasteful not to get ahead while I had the energy. Looking back, that’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t remember people saying, like, “You’re talking too much” or anything that obvious. I may have been more… animated? Like when I was talking about the portfolio redesign or client stuff, I could go on for a while because I had all these connected ideas in my head and I wanted to explain the whole chain of why each thing mattered.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as talking more than usual, or if I was just excited and kind of over-explaining. I do that sometimes anyway, especially with design work, because I’ll start with one thought and then realize there are five related pieces of context. So… maybe a little? But I can’t say for sure that I was noticeably more talkative in a way other people pointed out.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or almost excessively cheerful?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not cheerful in a normal “I’m having a good week” way, exactly. Earlier this month there were moments where I felt almost electric, like everything was suddenly possible and I was just… bright, or switched on. I remember feeling really upbeat, like I could handle anything and people should just get on board because I had momentum.\n\nBut it wasn’t consistently pleasant, which is part of what confused me. I’d be in that really energized, optimistic state, and then if something tiny slowed me down, I’d get sharply irritated. It felt excessive. Not like my usual mood.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that unusually energized, upbeat-or-irritable period last?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pin it down to an exact number of days, because it didn’t feel like one clean block with a clear start and stop. And it wasn’t some tiny optional meeting either—it mattered. Afterward I felt awful, like I’d made myself look unreliable or unprofessional.\n\nAnd with relationships, yes. I’ve been avoiding friends and family more than usual. Not because I don’t care about them, but because I feel embarrassed. I feel like I’ve been so all over the place lately—hard to pin down, intense, unreliable, kind of scattered—and I don’t want to have to explain it or see their reaction. So I’ve let messages sit, I’ve put off calls, and then the longer I avoid people, the more ashamed I feel.\n\nSo, yes. I wouldn’t say my life completely fell apart, but it’s caused real problems.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this past month, did you ever hear or see things that other people couldn’t, or have beliefs that others said were clearly not true?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / web_or_digital_interface_designer / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t had anything like hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there, at least not in a way that I recognized. And I don’t think anyone has told me I was believing something obviously untrue.

I’ve definitely felt really keyed up and maybe more intense in how I interpret things, but not like… I don’t know, not like I thought the TV was sending me messages or that people were plotting against me or anything like that. If anything, I worry afterward that I was being dramatic or overreading situations, but I don’t think it crossed into seeing or hearing things other people couldn’t, or having some fixed belief everyone was telling me was false.

It felt more like I genuinely believed I was fine, like I didn’t need more sleep and it would be wasteful not to get ahead while I had the energy. It was more… I knew things felt intense and not like my usual self, but I don’t think anyone around me was treating it as an emergency psychiatric situation.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did these symptoms cause serious problems in your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m kind of embarrassed to say that, because part of me wants to say I’ve been “handling it,” but that’s not really true.\n\nWork definitely took a hit. I missed a design review, which is not like me. I mean, I’m usually the person who’s overly aware of calendars and deadlines, and this time I just… I don’t know, I was scattered enough that I didn’t show up the way I was supposed to. And it wasn’t some tiny optional meeting either—it mattered. Afterward I felt awful, like I’d made myself look unreliable or unprofessional.\n\nAnd with relationships, yes. I’ve been avoiding friends and family more than usual. Not because I don’t care about them, but because I feel embarrassed. I feel like I’ve been so all over the place lately—hard to pin down, intense, unreliable, kind of scattered—and I don’t want to have to explain it or see their reaction. I wouldn’t say my life completely fell apart, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did you ever hear or see things that other people couldn’t, or have beliefs that others said were clearly not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t had anything like hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there, at least not in a way that I recognized. And I don’t think anyone has told me I was believing something obviously untrue.\n\nI’ve definitely felt really keyed up and maybe more intense in how I interpret things, but not like… I don’t know, not like I thought the TV was sending me messages or that people were plotting against me or anything like that. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it was impossible in the sense that I couldn’t get out of bed or couldn’t do anything at all, but… it did get to the point where I wasn’t functioning like I normally do.

The clearest example is that I missed a design review at work, and that felt pretty serious to me. I’m usually very on top of things like that—meetings, deadlines, making sure I’m prepared—and I just wasn’t. I was scattered, and I didn’t follow through on something that was part of my actual job, not just some minor thing I could shrug off.

And outside of work, I’ve been avoiding friends and family because I feel embarrassed about how I’ve seemed lately. Like I’ve been unreliable or hard to keep up with, and then I don’t want to face people or explain myself, so I pull back more. So day-to-day, no, I wasn’t completely unable to function, but it definitely interfered with responsibilities and relationships in a way that felt really unlike me.

And it wasn’t some tiny optional meeting either—it mattered. Afterward I felt awful, like I’d made myself look unreliable or unprofessional.\n\nAnd with relationships, yes. I’ve been avoiding friends and family more than usual. Not because I don’t care about them, but because I feel embarrassed. I feel like I’ve been so all over the place lately—hard to pin down, intense, unreliable, kind of scattered—and I don’t want to have to explain it or see their reaction. So I’ve let messages sit, I’ve put off calls, and then the longer I avoid people, the more ashamed I feel.\n\nSo, yes. I wouldn’t say my life completely fell apart, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did you ever hear or see things that other people couldn’t, or have beliefs that others said were clearly not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t had anything like hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there, at least not in a way that I recognized. And I don’t think anyone has told me I was believing something obviously untrue.\n\nI’ve definitely felt really keyed up and maybe more intense in how I interpret things, but not like… I don’t know, not like I thought the TV was sending me messages or that people were plotting against me or anything like that. If anything, I worry afterward that I was being dramatic or overreading situations, but I don’t think it crossed into seeing or hearing things other people couldn’t, or having some fixed belief everyone was telling me was false.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did there come a point when your symptoms made it impossible for you to function in your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was impossible in the sense that I couldn’t get out of bed or couldn’t do anything at all, but… it did get to the point where I wasn’t functioning like I normally do.\n\nThe clearest example is that I missed a design review at work, and that felt pretty serious to me. I’m usually very on top of things like that—meetings, deadlines, making sure I’m prepared—and I just wasn’t. I was scattered, and I didn’t follow through on something that was part of my actual job, not just some minor thing I could shrug off.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding friends and family because I feel embarrassed about how I’ve seemed lately. Like I’ve been unreliable or hard to keep up with, and then I don’t want to face people or explain myself, so I pull back more. So day-to-day, no, I wasn’t completely unable to function, but it definitely interfered with responsibilities and relationships in a way that felt really unlike me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day, or lost interest in almost everything?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, lately I’ve been waking up with this really heavy, gray feeling in my chest, like before anything has even happened that day. The apartment will be quiet, nothing is technically wrong, and I’ll already feel like I’m on the edge of crying.

It’s embarrassing because sometimes it’s over nothing—like I’ll see a coffee mug sitting in the sink and it just hits me way harder than it should. It’s not really about the mug, obviously, but in the moment it feels like this tiny proof that everything is too much, or that I’m too much, and I just feel sad in this very physical way.

So… yes, there have been stretches where it’s felt like that most of the day, nearly every day. Not just a bad mood for an afternoon. More like I wake up already under it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… I think what finally made me make the appointment is that I noticed I went from barely being able to just settle into my new apartment—like, boxes still around, basic things not really organized—to suddenly saying yes to everything.\n\nI’m a web and interface designer, and it was like out of nowhere I was agreeing to mentoring sessions, taking on extra client prototype work, and then deciding I needed to completely redo my entire portfolio at the same time. Not just update it a little—like redesign the whole thing, rethink my case studies, fix every project, make it all perfect. It felt like I had to fix my entire life in one sprint, if that makes sense.\n\nAt first it felt productive, like maybe I was finally getting on top of things, but then I kind of stepped back and thought, okay, this is… a lot. I hadn’t even really unpacked properly, and suddenly I was stacking all these goals and commitments on myself like they were urgent. That’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: During this period, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That was actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, something is off, because normally if I only sleep a few hours, I’m useless the next day. But during that stretch, there were several nights where I slept maybe three or four hours—like I’d go to bed really late because I was working on something or thinking through what I wanted to do next—and then I’d wake up really early and feel like, “Great, I’m up, I should use this time.”\n\nIt didn’t feel like being tired and pushing through it. It felt more like I genuinely believed I was fine, like I didn’t need more sleep and it would be wasteful not to get ahead while I had the energy. Looking back, that’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t remember people saying, like, “You’re talking too much” or anything that obvious. I may have been more… animated? Like when I was talking about the portfolio redesign or client stuff, I could go on for a while because I had all these connected ideas in my head and I wanted to explain the whole chain of why each thing mattered.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as talking more than usual, or if I was just excited and kind of over-explaining. I do that sometimes anyway, especially with design work, because I’ll start with one thought and then realize there are five related pieces of context. So… maybe a little? But I can’t say for sure that I was noticeably more talkative in a way other people pointed out.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or almost excessively cheerful?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not cheerful in a normal “I’m having a good week” way, exactly. Earlier this month there were moments where I felt almost electric, like everything was suddenly possible and I was just… bright, or switched on. I remember feeling really upbeat, like I could handle anything and people should just get on board because I had momentum.\n\nBut it wasn’t consistently pleasant, which is part of what confused me. I’d be in that really energized, optimistic state, and then if something tiny slowed me down, I’d get sharply irritated. Like I snapped at a contractor over a delay that, looking back, was not that dramatic. And I snapped at my sister too—over something small, just not responding fast enough or not understanding what I was trying to do—and in the moment it felt like they were personally blocking me, like they were obstacles in my way.\n\nSo… yes, there was this unusually upbeat feeling, but it also tipped really quickly into irritability. It felt excessive. Not like my usual mood.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that unusually energized, upbeat-or-irritable period last?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pin it down to an exact number of days, because it didn’t feel like one clean block with a clear start and stop. But the whole intense up-and-down pattern has basically been going on since I moved into the apartment, so… about a month.\n\nThere were probably days in there that felt more “normal” than others, or at least less extreme, but since the move it’s been this ongoing thing where my energy and mood have felt turned way up compared to my usual. So if you’re asking about the broader stretch, I’d say roughly a month, not just a couple of days.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did you have to be hospitalized or did anyone think you needed emergency psychiatric care because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Not that I’m aware of, no. I mean, nobody said to me, “You need to go to the hospital,” or “This is an emergency,” at least not in any direct way that I remember.\n\nI didn’t have some clear moment where things escalated to that level, like someone calling emergency services or taking me somewhere. It was more… I knew things felt intense and not like my usual self, but I don’t think anyone around me was treating it as an emergency psychiatric situation.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did these symptoms cause serious problems in your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m kind of embarrassed to say that, because part of me wants to say I’ve been “handling it,” but that’s not really true.\n\nWork definitely took a hit. I missed a design review, which is not like me. I mean, I’m usually the person who’s overly aware of calendars and deadlines, and this time I just… I don’t know, I was scattered enough that I didn’t show up the way I was supposed to. And it wasn’t some tiny optional meeting either—it mattered. Afterward I felt awful, like I’d made myself look unreliable or unprofessional.\n\nAnd with relationships, yes. I’ve been avoiding friends and family more than usual. Not because I don’t care about them, but because I feel embarrassed. I feel like I’ve been so all over the place lately—hard to pin down, intense, unreliable, kind of scattered—and I don’t want to have to explain it or see their reaction. So I’ve let messages sit, I’ve put off calls, and then the longer I avoid people, the more ashamed I feel.\n\nSo, yes. I wouldn’t say my life completely fell apart, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did you ever hear or see things that other people couldn’t, or have beliefs that others said were clearly not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. And I don’t think anyone has told me I was believing something obviously untrue.\n\nI’ve definitely felt really keyed up and maybe more intense in how I interpret things, but not like… I don’t know, not like I thought the TV was sending me messages or that people were plotting against me or anything like that. If anything, I worry afterward that I was being dramatic or overreading situations, but I don’t think it crossed into seeing or hearing things other people couldn’t, or having some fixed belief everyone was telling me was false.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did there come a point when your symptoms made it impossible for you to function in your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was impossible in the sense that I couldn’t get out of bed or couldn’t do anything at all, but… it did get to the point where I wasn’t functioning like I normally do.\n\nThe clearest example is that I missed a design review at work, and that felt pretty serious to me. Yes. Before this past month, yes. It’s been… honestly, the heavier stretches started after the separation, which was about 11 months ago now. I don’t think it was constant every single day for the whole 11 months, but there were definitely periods where it lasted at least a couple of weeks, where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day.

Looking back, that’s not really normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t remember people saying, like, “You’re talking too much” or anything that obvious. I may have been more… animated? Like when I was talking about the portfolio redesign or client stuff, I could go on for a while because I had all these connected ideas in my head and I wanted to explain the whole chain of why each thing mattered.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as talking more than usual, or if I was just excited and kind of over-explaining. I do that sometimes anyway, especially with design work, because I’ll start with one thought and then realize there are five related pieces of context. So… maybe a little? But I can’t say for sure that I was noticeably more talkative in a way other people pointed out.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch, did you feel unusually high, euphoric, or almost excessively cheerful?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Not cheerful in a normal “I’m having a good week” way, exactly. Earlier this month there were moments where I felt almost electric, like everything was suddenly possible and I was just… bright, or switched on. I remember feeling really upbeat, like I could handle anything and people should just get on board because I had momentum.\n\nBut it wasn’t consistently pleasant, which is part of what confused me. I’d be in that really energized, optimistic state, and then if something tiny slowed me down, I’d get sharply irritated. Like I snapped at a contractor over a delay that, looking back, was not that dramatic. And I snapped at my sister too—over something small, just not responding fast enough or not understanding what I was trying to do—and in the moment it felt like they were personally blocking me, like they were obstacles in my way.\n\nSo… yes, there was this unusually upbeat feeling, but it also tipped really quickly into irritability. It felt excessive. Not like my usual mood.\n\nDoctor: About how many days in a row did that unusually energized, upbeat-or-irritable period last?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pin it down to an exact number of days, because it didn’t feel like one clean block with a clear start and stop. But the whole intense up-and-down pattern has basically been going on since I moved into the apartment, so… about a month.\n\nThere were probably days in there that felt more “normal” than others, or at least less extreme, but since the move it’s been this ongoing thing where my energy and mood have felt turned way up compared to my usual. So if you’re asking about the broader stretch, I’d say roughly a month, not just a couple of days.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did you have to be hospitalized or did anyone think you needed emergency psychiatric care because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: Not that I’m aware of, no. I mean, nobody said to me, “You need to go to the hospital,” or “This is an emergency,” at least not in any direct way that I remember.\n\nI didn’t have some clear moment where things escalated to that level, like someone calling emergency services or taking me somewhere. It was more… I knew things felt intense and not like my usual self, but I don’t think anyone around me was treating it as an emergency psychiatric situation.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did these symptoms cause serious problems in your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m kind of embarrassed to say that, because part of me wants to say I’ve been “handling it,” but that’s not really true.\n\nWork definitely took a hit. I missed a design review, which is not like me. I mean, I’m usually the person who’s overly aware of calendars and deadlines, and this time I just… I don’t know, I was scattered enough that I didn’t show up the way I was supposed to. And it wasn’t some tiny optional meeting either—it mattered. Afterward I felt awful, like I’d made myself look unreliable or unprofessional.\n\nAnd with relationships, yes. I’ve been avoiding friends and family more than usual. Not because I don’t care about them, but because I feel embarrassed. I feel like I’ve been so all over the place lately—hard to pin down, intense, unreliable, kind of scattered—and I don’t want to have to explain it or see their reaction. So I’ve let messages sit, I’ve put off calls, and then the longer I avoid people, the more ashamed I feel.\n\nSo, yes. I wouldn’t say my life completely fell apart, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did you ever hear or see things that other people couldn’t, or have beliefs that others said were clearly not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t had anything like hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there, at least not in a way that I recognized. And I don’t think anyone has told me I was believing something obviously untrue.\n\nI’ve definitely felt really keyed up and maybe more intense in how I interpret things, but not like… I don’t know, not like I thought the TV was sending me messages or that people were plotting against me or anything like that. If anything, I worry afterward that I was being dramatic or overreading situations, but I don’t think it crossed into seeing or hearing things other people couldn’t, or having some fixed belief everyone was telling me was false.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did there come a point when your symptoms made it impossible for you to function in your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was impossible in the sense that I couldn’t get out of bed or couldn’t do anything at all, but… it did get to the point where I wasn’t functioning like I normally do.\n\nThe clearest example is that I missed a design review at work, and that felt pretty serious to me. I’m usually very on top of things like that—meetings, deadlines, making sure I’m prepared—and I just wasn’t. I was scattered, and I didn’t follow through on something that was part of my actual job, not just some minor thing I could shrug off.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding friends and family because I feel embarrassed about how I’ve seemed lately. Like I’ve been unreliable or hard to keep up with, and then I don’t want to face people or explain myself, so I pull back more. So day-to-day, no, I wasn’t completely unable to function, but it definitely interfered with responsibilities and relationships in a way that felt really unlike me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day, or lost interest in almost everything?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, lately I’ve been waking up with this really heavy, gray feeling in my chest, like before anything has even happened that day. The apartment will be quiet, nothing is technically wrong, and I’ll already feel like I’m on the edge of crying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because sometimes it’s over nothing—like I’ll see a coffee mug sitting in the sink and it just hits me way harder than it should. It’s not really about the mug, obviously, but in the moment it feels like this tiny proof that everything is too much, or that I’m too much, and I just feel sad in this very physical way.\n\nSo… yes, there have been stretches where it’s felt like that most of the day, nearly every day. Not just a bad mood for an afternoon. More like I wake up already under it.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before this past month, yes. It’s been… honestly, the heavier stretches started after the separation, which was about 11 months ago now. I don’t think it was constant every single day for the whole 11 months, but there were definitely periods where it lasted at least a couple of weeks, where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day.\n\nIt kind of came in waves, but the waves were long enough that it wasn’t just, like, “I had a bad weekend” or “I’m sad about the breakup today.” It felt more settled in than that. I mean, nobody said to me, “You need to go to the hospital,” or “This is an emergency,” at least not in any direct way that I remember.\n\nI didn’t have some clear moment where things escalated to that level, like someone calling emergency services or taking me somewhere. It was more… I knew things felt intense and not like my usual self, but I don’t think anyone around me was treating it as an emergency psychiatric situation.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did these symptoms cause serious problems in your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I’m kind of embarrassed to say that, because part of me wants to say I’ve been “handling it,” but that’s not really true.\n\nWork definitely took a hit. I missed a design review, which is not like me. I mean, I’m usually the person who’s overly aware of calendars and deadlines, and this time I just… I don’t know, I was scattered enough that I didn’t show up the way I was supposed to. And it wasn’t some tiny optional meeting either—it mattered. Afterward I felt awful, like I’d made myself look unreliable or unprofessional.\n\nAnd with relationships, yes. I’ve been avoiding friends and family more than usual. Not because I don’t care about them, but because I feel embarrassed. I feel like I’ve been so all over the place lately—hard to pin down, intense, unreliable, kind of scattered—and I don’t want to have to explain it or see their reaction. So I’ve let messages sit, I’ve put off calls, and then the longer I avoid people, the more ashamed I feel.\n\nSo, yes. I wouldn’t say my life completely fell apart, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did you ever hear or see things that other people couldn’t, or have beliefs that others said were clearly not true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… I haven’t had anything like hearing voices or seeing things that weren’t there, at least not in a way that I recognized. And I don’t think anyone has told me I was believing something obviously untrue.\n\nI’ve definitely felt really keyed up and maybe more intense in how I interpret things, but not like… I don’t know, not like I thought the TV was sending me messages or that people were plotting against me or anything like that. If anything, I worry afterward that I was being dramatic or overreading situations, but I don’t think it crossed into seeing or hearing things other people couldn’t, or having some fixed belief everyone was telling me was false.\n\nDoctor: During this past month, did there come a point when your symptoms made it impossible for you to function in your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was impossible in the sense that I couldn’t get out of bed or couldn’t do anything at all, but… it did get to the point where I wasn’t functioning like I normally do.\n\nThe clearest example is that I missed a design review at work, and that felt pretty serious to me. I’m usually very on top of things like that—meetings, deadlines, making sure I’m prepared—and I just wasn’t. I was scattered, and I didn’t follow through on something that was part of my actual job, not just some minor thing I could shrug off.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been avoiding friends and family because I feel embarrassed about how I’ve seemed lately. The apartment will be quiet, nothing is technically wrong, and I’ll already feel like I’m on the edge of crying.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because sometimes it’s over nothing—like I’ll see a coffee mug sitting in the sink and it just hits me way harder than it should. Not just a bad mood for an afternoon. More like I wake up already under it.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before this past month, yes. It’s been… honestly, the heavier stretches started after the separation, which was about 11 months ago now. I don’t think it was constant every single day for the whole 11 months, but there were definitely periods where it lasted at least a couple of weeks, where I was depressed most of the day nearly every day.\n\nIt kind of came in waves, but the waves were long enough that it wasn’t just, like, “I had a bad weekend” or “I’m sad about the breakup today.” It felt more settled in than that. I’m trying to think back, and I don’t want to say yes just because I’ve had times where I was busy or stressed or stayed up too late working on something.\n\nBefore this past month, I don’t really have a clear example in my mind of a distinct period where I felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep in that same kind of noticeable way. I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well, or stretches where life felt more intense, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as this clear “energized” period.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure, but nothing obvious is coming to mind.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this past month, were there days when you felt depressed and unusually energized at the same time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / web_or_digital_interface_designer / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Activity\nmanifestation: \"There were nights when I couldn't sit still, moving furniture, labeling storage bins, tweaking mockups, and pacing through the apartment because stopping felt impossible.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… actually, that’s a good way to put it, even though it sounds kind of contradictory. There were days — and especially nights — where I felt really low, like emotionally just awful, but at the same time I couldn’t settle down at all.

I’d be in the apartment feeling depressed and overwhelmed, but instead of just lying there, I’d start doing things almost compulsively. Like moving furniture around at midnight because suddenly the layout felt wrong, or labeling storage bins and reorganizing things that probably did not need to be reorganized right then. I’d open my laptop and keep tweaking design mockups, tiny little spacing changes or colors, over and over, even when I knew I should stop.

And I remember pacing through the apartment a lot. Not even going anywhere, just room to room, because sitting still felt impossible. It wasn’t like I felt happy or good exactly. It was more like I felt miserable but wired, like if I stopped moving everything would catch up with me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, yes, during that same stretch, I wasn’t just energized or wired in some separate, positive way. A lot of the time I still felt depressed most of the day.

Lately I’ll wake up and it’s like there’s this heavy, gray feeling sitting in my chest before I’ve even done anything. And it doesn’t necessarily need a reason, which is part of what makes it feel so unsettling. The apartment can be completely quiet, nothing actively bad happening in that moment, and I’ll still feel like I’m right on the edge of crying.

Like, something tiny will set it off — or almost set it off. I’ll see a coffee mug sitting in the sink and feel this ridiculous wave of sadness or heaviness, like it means something bigger than it does. And I know logically it’s just a mug, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m not managing, or that everything is too much.

So yes, even on the days when I had that restless energy and didn’t seem to need as much sleep, the depression was still there through most of the day. It was more like being sad and wound up at the same time.
